Nice to meet you too. Um, well, I guess what made me bring it up and come in today is that for the past few weeks, my eating has just been... off. Like, I’ll skip breakfast and lunch without even meaning to. 

Actually, speaking of lunch, I used to go to this amazing deli right by the campus back when I was still taking classes. They had this pastrami sandwich that was just unreal. I haven't been there in, wow, years now, but man, I still think about that place. The guy who ran it was from Brooklyn, I think, and he always gave you extra pickles. 

But anyway, yeah, lately I just forget to eat during the day. And then I’ll just pick at dinner, even when my wife cooks. She made her meatloaf the other night—she uses this brown sugar glaze her mom taught her, which is usually really good, but I just couldn't really eat it. And I've been noticing my jeans are starting to feel pretty loose lately, so... yeah, I guess that's why I'm here.

Yeah, I guess you could say that. Most days lately, I just wake up with this really heavy, flat feeling. It's like... I don't know, like the apartment is just way too quiet, you know? Even picking up my guitar doesn't really lift me.

Um, well, this current low stretch has been going on for about three weeks now. I guess it kind of started right after my wife settled into her new full-time work schedule. 

She used to work part-time, just three days a week, which was nice because we'd have these long weekends together. We actually used to take these little road trips up to the mountains on her days off. There's this one cabin we rented a couple times near Big Bear. The guy who owns it built it himself back in the 70s, and it has this incredible wraparound deck made of redwood. He told us he sourced all the timber locally, which is pretty cool. We'd sit out there in the mornings with our coffee and just watch the Steller's jays hopping around in the pines. They're such loud birds, you know? Like, they have this really harsh call, almost like a screech, but their blue feathers are just so striking against the green trees. 

But yeah, anyway, she's full-time now, five days a week, so she's gone a lot more. And that's... that's right around when this started, about three weeks ago.

We actually used to take these little road trips up to the mountains on her days off. There's this one cabin we rented a couple times near Big Bear. The guy who owns it built it himself back in the 70s, and it has this incredible wraparound deck made of redwood. He told us he sourced all the timber locally, which is pretty cool. We'd sit out there in the mornings with our coffee and just watch the Steller's jays hopping around in the pines. They're such loud birds, you know? I mean... maybe? It's hard to say if it's *mainly* because of that. I definitely miss having her around as much, but I don't know if that's the whole reason.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know if her being home would just instantly fix it, honestly. It's like... most days lately, I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling. Even if she was here, the apartment just feels too quiet. I can't even pick up my guitar to try and lift my mood. 

I actually bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but it has this really beautiful sunburst finish. The guy who sold it to me, he was missing his left index finger. He said he lost it in a woodworking accident back in the 90s when he was building custom cabinets. He still played, though! He showed me this crazy slide technique he developed to compensate for the missing finger. It sounded kind of like old Delta blues, you know? Robert Johnson style. I tried looking up some tutorials on YouTube to learn how to play with a slide, but you have to use this special glass or metal tube on your finger, and it just clanks against the frets when I try it. 

But yeah, anyway... even the guitar doesn't really help right now. Just this heavy feeling every morning, whether she's here or not.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, actually, I really have. It's been super frustrating. Like, when I try to work on some code or even arrange a new song on the computer, I just get stuck. I'll keep rereading the exact same line of code, or replaying the same eight bars of music over and over, and I just can't decide what to do next. My brain just kind of stalls out. 

Speaking of coding, I actually started learning Python a couple years ago because I wanted to build this automated watering system for my wife's houseplants. She has this massive Monstera plant that she named 'Seymour' after that movie Little Shop of Horrors. Have you seen that? The original one with Rick Moranis is a classic, but the Broadway musical version has this totally different ending where the plant actually eats everybody and takes over the world. It's way darker. Anyway, Seymour the plant is huge, and I bought all these little moisture sensors on Amazon to stick in the soil. They were supposed to connect to a Raspberry Pi mini-computer, which is this tiny little circuit board that costs like thirty bucks. The guy who invented them actually wanted to make computing affordable for kids in developing countries, which is such a noble idea. 

But yeah... I just stare at the screen now. I can't focus enough to actually write the script to make the sensors talk to the water pump. I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.

Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. 

I was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. 

But anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.

Even picking up my guitar doesn't really lift me. \n\nActually, I bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but the action on it is incredible. The guy who sold it to me was this old blues player who claimed he once jammed with B.B. King. I mean, who knows if that's true, but he sure could play. He showed me this crazy pentatonic lick that I still practice sometimes. Or, well, I used to practice it. Lately, it just sits in the corner on its stand, collecting dust. But yeah, to answer your question, I've definitely been feeling pretty down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to die or hurt yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't really thought about anything like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I don't want to hurt myself. \n\nMy wife actually asks me that sometimes, especially when I get in these funks. She's a nurse, actually, over at the pediatric clinic on 4th street. It's a really nice facility, they just renovated the whole waiting room with this under-the-sea theme. They have these big murals of sea turtles and coral reefs, and there's this one huge octopus that takes up a whole wall. The artist who painted it is local, I think she has a studio in the arts district. We went to an art walk down there last summer and saw some of her other stuff, mostly landscapes, but really vibrant colors. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any thoughts about dying. Just... just feeling really flat.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Um, well, this current low stretch has been going on for about three weeks now. I guess it kind of started right after my wife settled into her new full-time work schedule. \n\nShe used to work part-time, just three days a week, which was nice because we'd have these long weekends together. We actually used to take these little road trips up to the mountains on her days off. There's this one cabin we rented a couple times near Big Bear. The guy who owns it built it himself back in the 70s, and it has this incredible wraparound deck made of redwood. He told us he sourced all the timber locally, which is pretty cool. We'd sit out there in the mornings with our coffee and just watch the Steller's jays hopping around in the pines. They're such loud birds, you know? Like, they have this really harsh call, almost like a screech, but their blue feathers are just so striking against the green trees. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, she's full-time now, five days a week, so she's gone a lot more. And that's... that's right around when this started, about three weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your low mood is mainly because your wife is gone more now?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? It's hard to say if it's *mainly* because of that. I definitely miss having her around as much, but I don't know if that's the whole reason. \n\nIt is weird being in the apartment by myself all day, though. We live in this complex that was built back in the 60s, and it's got this really interesting mid-century modern architecture. The roofline has these sharp angles, and there's this central courtyard with a pool that nobody ever uses. The pool guy comes every Tuesday, like clockwork. His name is Dave, older guy, always wears this faded Dodgers hat. He told me once he went to the World Series back in '88, the year Kirk Gibson hit that famous home run. He said the stadium just absolutely erupted, like you could feel the concrete shaking under your feet. I've never been to a baseball game myself, honestly. Just never really got into sports. My dad tried to get me into Little League when I was a kid, but I was always more interested in drawing in the dirt in the outfield. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, my wife's schedule. Yeah, I'm not totally sure if that's the main cause of the low mood, to be honest. It just happened around the same time.\n\nDoctor: If your wife were home with you more again, do you think your mood would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know if her being home would just instantly fix it, honestly. It's like... most days lately, I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling. Even if she was here, the apartment just feels too quiet. I can't even pick up my guitar to try and lift my mood. \n\nI actually bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but it has this really beautiful sunburst finish. The guy who sold it to me, he was missing his left index finger. He said he lost it in a woodworking accident back in the 90s when he was building custom cabinets. He still played, though! He showed me this crazy slide technique he developed to compensate for the missing finger. It sounded kind of like old Delta blues, you know? Robert Johnson style. I tried looking up some tutorials on YouTube to learn how to play with a slide, but you have to use this special glass or metal tube on your finger, and it just clanks against the frets when I try it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... even the guitar doesn't really help right now. Just this heavy feeling every morning, whether she's here or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It's been super frustrating. Like, when I try to work on some code or even arrange a new song on the computer, I just get stuck. I'll keep rereading the exact same line of code, or replaying the same eight bars of music over and over, and I just can't decide what to do next. My brain just kind of stalls out. \n\nSpeaking of coding, I actually started learning Python a couple years ago because I wanted to build this automated watering system for my wife's houseplants. She has this massive Monstera plant that she named 'Seymour' after that movie Little Shop of Horrors. Have you seen that? The original one with Rick Moranis is a classic, but the Broadway musical version has this totally different ending where the plant actually eats everybody and takes over the world. It's way darker. Anyway, Seymour the plant is huge, and I bought all these little moisture sensors on Amazon to stick in the soil. They were supposed to connect to a Raspberry Pi mini-computer, which is this tiny little circuit board that costs like thirty bucks. The guy who invented them actually wanted to make computing affordable for kids in developing countries, which is such a noble idea. \n\nBut yeah... I just stare at the screen now. I can't focus enough to actually write the script to make the sensors talk to the water pump. I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. \n\nI was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you only slept three or four hours, did you feel unusually upbeat or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really did. Before this whole low spell hit, I had this run where I was just... unusually fired up. Like, really upbeat, but also kind of on edge? I felt like I was totally on a roll with everything, but I'd get so easily annoyed if anyone interrupted me. I remember snapping at my wife because she asked me what I wanted for dinner while I was in the middle of re-wiring my pedalboard. \n\nI actually use this really specific power supply for my pedals, the Voodoo Lab Pedal Power 2 Plus. It's supposed to isolate the power to each pedal so you don't get that annoying ground loop hum. Hum is the worst when you're trying to record. I spent like three hours watching this documentary on Netflix about the history of electricity and the war of the currents between Edison and Tesla just to understand alternating current better. Tesla was such a genius, you know? He died completely broke in a hotel room in New York, feeding pigeons. He had this one specific white pigeon that he said he loved like a man loves a woman. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. I felt great, but I was definitely irritable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, actually, my wife pointed that out. She told me I was talking way faster than usual. Like, I'd be going on and on, jumping from talking about these new audio plugins I wanted to buy, straight into this business plan I had for a local artist collective, and then onto something else. Speaking of local artists, there's this guy down the street who does these massive metal sculptures out of old car parts. He told me he sources all the parts from this junkyard out by the highway. Junkyards are fascinating places, honestly. You walk through them and it's just rows and rows of these rusted-out machines that used to be someone's pride and joy, you know? Someone probably drove that Beetle off the lot in 1972, feeling like a million bucks, and now it's a bug leg in some guy's yard. I actually used to drive a Beetle when I was in my early twenties. It was this awful mustard yellow color, and the heater was permanently stuck on, which was miserable in the summer. I tried to fix it myself once, watched a bunch of YouTube tutorials, but I ended up snapping a cable and had to drive with the windows down for two years... 

But yeah, I was definitely talking a lot. Just moving from one thing to the next, and my wife was just kind of staring at me trying to keep up.

No, no, I wasn't hospitalized or anything like that. I mean, I was just at home, you know, working on my stuff. Hospitals kind of freak me out anyway, honestly. And that's... that's right around when this started, about three weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your low mood is mainly because your wife is gone more now?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? It's hard to say if it's *mainly* because of that. I definitely miss having her around as much, but I don't know if that's the whole reason. \n\nIt is weird being in the apartment by myself all day, though. We live in this complex that was built back in the 60s, and it's got this really interesting mid-century modern architecture. The roofline has these sharp angles, and there's this central courtyard with a pool that nobody ever uses. The pool guy comes every Tuesday, like clockwork. His name is Dave, older guy, always wears this faded Dodgers hat. He told me once he went to the World Series back in '88, the year Kirk Gibson hit that famous home run. He said the stadium just absolutely erupted, like you could feel the concrete shaking under your feet. I've never been to a baseball game myself, honestly. Just never really got into sports. My dad tried to get me into Little League when I was a kid, but I was always more interested in drawing in the dirt in the outfield. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, my wife's schedule. Yeah, I'm not totally sure if that's the main cause of the low mood, to be honest. It just happened around the same time.\n\nDoctor: If your wife were home with you more again, do you think your mood would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know if her being home would just instantly fix it, honestly. It's like... most days lately, I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling. Even if she was here, the apartment just feels too quiet. I can't even pick up my guitar to try and lift my mood. \n\nI actually bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but it has this really beautiful sunburst finish. The guy who sold it to me, he was missing his left index finger. He said he lost it in a woodworking accident back in the 90s when he was building custom cabinets. He still played, though! He showed me this crazy slide technique he developed to compensate for the missing finger. It sounded kind of like old Delta blues, you know? Robert Johnson style. I tried looking up some tutorials on YouTube to learn how to play with a slide, but you have to use this special glass or metal tube on your finger, and it just clanks against the frets when I try it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... even the guitar doesn't really help right now. Just this heavy feeling every morning, whether she's here or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It's been super frustrating. Like, when I try to work on some code or even arrange a new song on the computer, I just get stuck. I'll keep rereading the exact same line of code, or replaying the same eight bars of music over and over, and I just can't decide what to do next. My brain just kind of stalls out. \n\nSpeaking of coding, I actually started learning Python a couple years ago because I wanted to build this automated watering system for my wife's houseplants. She has this massive Monstera plant that she named 'Seymour' after that movie Little Shop of Horrors. Have you seen that? The original one with Rick Moranis is a classic, but the Broadway musical version has this totally different ending where the plant actually eats everybody and takes over the world. It's way darker. Anyway, Seymour the plant is huge, and I bought all these little moisture sensors on Amazon to stick in the soil. They were supposed to connect to a Raspberry Pi mini-computer, which is this tiny little circuit board that costs like thirty bucks. The guy who invented them actually wanted to make computing affordable for kids in developing countries, which is such a noble idea. \n\nBut yeah... I just stare at the screen now. I can't focus enough to actually write the script to make the sensors talk to the water pump. I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. \n\nI was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you only slept three or four hours, did you feel unusually upbeat or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really did. Before this whole low spell hit, I had this run where I was just... unusually fired up. Like, really upbeat, but also kind of on edge? I felt like I was totally on a roll with everything, but I'd get so easily annoyed if anyone interrupted me. I remember snapping at my wife because she asked me what I wanted for dinner while I was in the middle of re-wiring my pedalboard. \n\nI actually use this really specific power supply for my pedals, the Voodoo Lab Pedal Power 2 Plus. It's supposed to isolate the power to each pedal so you don't get that annoying ground loop hum. Hum is the worst when you're trying to record. I spent like three hours watching this documentary on Netflix about the history of electricity and the war of the currents between Edison and Tesla just to understand alternating current better. Tesla was such a genius, you know? He died completely broke in a hotel room in New York, feeding pigeons. He had this one specific white pigeon that he said he loved like a man loves a woman. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. I felt great, but I was definitely irritable.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you find yourself talking a lot more than usual or feeling like you couldn't stop talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, my wife pointed that out. She told me I was talking way faster than usual. Like, I'd be going on and on, jumping from talking about these new audio plugins I wanted to buy, straight into this business plan I had for a local artist collective, and then onto something else. I could kind of tell I wasn't really giving her much room to answer or even get a word in. \n\nSpeaking of local artists, there's this guy down the street who does these massive metal sculptures out of old car parts. He's got this one in his front yard that looks like a giant praying mantis made entirely out of Volkswagen Beetle fenders. It's actually really impressive. He told me he sources all the parts from this junkyard out by the highway. Junkyards are fascinating places, honestly. You walk through them and it's just rows and rows of these rusted-out machines that used to be someone's pride and joy, you know? Someone probably drove that Beetle off the lot in 1972, feeling like a million bucks, and now it's a bug leg in some guy's yard. I actually used to drive a Beetle when I was in my early twenties. It was this awful mustard yellow color, and the heater was permanently stuck on, which was miserable in the summer. I tried to fix it myself once, watched a bunch of YouTube tutorials, but I ended up snapping a cable and had to drive with the windows down for two years... \n\nBut yeah, I was definitely talking a lot. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. I felt great, but I was definitely irritable.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you find yourself talking a lot more than usual or feeling like you couldn't stop talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, my wife pointed that out. Someone probably drove that Beetle off the lot in 1972, feeling like a million bucks, and now it's a bug leg in some guy's yard. I actually used to drive a Beetle when I was in my early twenties. It was this awful mustard yellow color, and the heater was permanently stuck on, which was miserable in the summer. I tried to fix it myself once, watched a bunch of YouTube tutorials, but I ended up snapping a cable and had to drive with the windows down for two years... \n\nBut yeah, I was definitely talking a lot. Just moving from one thing to the next, and my wife was just kind of staring at me trying to keep up.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you hospitalized?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wasn't hospitalized or anything like that. I mean, I was just at home, you know, working on my stuff. Hospitals kind of freak me out anyway, honestly. The lighting in those places is always so terrible. It's that awful fluorescent buzz, you know? It hums at like 60 hertz, which is right around a B-flat, and it just gets stuck in my head. I actually sampled a fluorescent light once for this ambient track I was making. I pitched it down two octaves and ran it through a massive reverb, and it sounded like this incredible drone. Reverb is such a powerful tool. My favorite is this old spring reverb tank I pulled out of a broken Hammond organ. Those organs are beasts. They weigh like 400 pounds. My buddy and I tried to move one up a flight of stairs once, and we almost dropped it on my foot. If that thing had landed on my toe, I probably would have ended up in the hospital, ironically enough. But no, for the energy thing, I didn't go to the hospital. No, no serious problems. Like I said, my wife got a little annoyed with me snapping at her and talking so fast, but it wasn't like a massive relationship-ending blowout or anything. We're solid. They had this pastrami sandwich that was just unreal. I haven't been there in, wow, years now, but man, I still think about that place. The guy who ran it was from Brooklyn, I think, and he always gave you extra pickles. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, lately I just forget to eat during the day. And then I’ll just pick at dinner, even when my wife cooks. She made her meatloaf the other night—she uses this brown sugar glaze her mom taught her, which is usually really good, but I just couldn't really eat it. And I've been noticing my jeans are starting to feel pretty loose lately, so... yeah, I guess that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. Most days lately, I just wake up with this really heavy, flat feeling. It's like... I don't know, like the apartment is just way too quiet, you know? Even picking up my guitar doesn't really lift me. \n\nActually, I bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but the action on it is incredible. The guy who sold it to me was this old blues player who claimed he once jammed with B.B. King. I mean, who knows if that's true, but he sure could play. He showed me this crazy pentatonic lick that I still practice sometimes. Or, well, I used to practice it. Lately, it just sits in the corner on its stand, collecting dust. But yeah, to answer your question, I've definitely been feeling pretty down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to die or hurt yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't really thought about anything like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I don't want to hurt myself. \n\nMy wife actually asks me that sometimes, especially when I get in these funks. She's a nurse, actually, over at the pediatric clinic on 4th street. It's a really nice facility, they just renovated the whole waiting room with this under-the-sea theme. They have these big murals of sea turtles and coral reefs, and there's this one huge octopus that takes up a whole wall. The artist who painted it is local, I think she has a studio in the arts district. We went to an art walk down there last summer and saw some of her other stuff, mostly landscapes, but really vibrant colors. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any thoughts about dying. Just... just feeling really flat.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Um, well, this current low stretch has been going on for about three weeks now. I guess it kind of started right after my wife settled into her new full-time work schedule. \n\nShe used to work part-time, just three days a week, which was nice because we'd have these long weekends together. We actually used to take these little road trips up to the mountains on her days off. There's this one cabin we rented a couple times near Big Bear. The guy who owns it built it himself back in the 70s, and it has this incredible wraparound deck made of redwood. He told us he sourced all the timber locally, which is pretty cool. We'd sit out there in the mornings with our coffee and just watch the Steller's jays hopping around in the pines. They're such loud birds, you know? Like, they have this really harsh call, almost like a screech, but their blue feathers are just so striking against the green trees. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, she's full-time now, five days a week, so she's gone a lot more. And that's... that's right around when this started, about three weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your low mood is mainly because your wife is gone more now?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? It's hard to say if it's *mainly* because of that. I definitely miss having her around as much, but I don't know if that's the whole reason. \n\nIt is weird being in the apartment by myself all day, though. We live in this complex that was built back in the 60s, and it's got this really interesting mid-century modern architecture. The roofline has these sharp angles, and there's this central courtyard with a pool that nobody ever uses. The pool guy comes every Tuesday, like clockwork. His name is Dave, older guy, always wears this faded Dodgers hat. He told me once he went to the World Series back in '88, the year Kirk Gibson hit that famous home run. He said the stadium just absolutely erupted, like you could feel the concrete shaking under your feet. I've never been to a baseball game myself, honestly. Just never really got into sports. My dad tried to get me into Little League when I was a kid, but I was always more interested in drawing in the dirt in the outfield. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, my wife's schedule. Yeah, I'm not totally sure if that's the main cause of the low mood, to be honest. It just happened around the same time.\n\nDoctor: If your wife were home with you more again, do you think your mood would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know if her being home would just instantly fix it, honestly. It's like... most days lately, I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling. Even if she was here, the apartment just feels too quiet. I can't even pick up my guitar to try and lift my mood. \n\nI actually bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but it has this really beautiful sunburst finish. The guy who sold it to me, he was missing his left index finger. He said he lost it in a woodworking accident back in the 90s when he was building custom cabinets. He still played, though! He showed me this crazy slide technique he developed to compensate for the missing finger. It sounded kind of like old Delta blues, you know? Robert Johnson style. I tried looking up some tutorials on YouTube to learn how to play with a slide, but you have to use this special glass or metal tube on your finger, and it just clanks against the frets when I try it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... even the guitar doesn't really help right now. Just this heavy feeling every morning, whether she's here or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It's been super frustrating. Like, when I try to work on some code or even arrange a new song on the computer, I just get stuck. I'll keep rereading the exact same line of code, or replaying the same eight bars of music over and over, and I just can't decide what to do next. My brain just kind of stalls out. \n\nSpeaking of coding, I actually started learning Python a couple years ago because I wanted to build this automated watering system for my wife's houseplants. She has this massive Monstera plant that she named 'Seymour' after that movie Little Shop of Horrors. Have you seen that? The original one with Rick Moranis is a classic, but the Broadway musical version has this totally different ending where the plant actually eats everybody and takes over the world. It's way darker. Anyway, Seymour the plant is huge, and I bought all these little moisture sensors on Amazon to stick in the soil. They were supposed to connect to a Raspberry Pi mini-computer, which is this tiny little circuit board that costs like thirty bucks. The guy who invented them actually wanted to make computing affordable for kids in developing countries, which is such a noble idea. \n\nBut yeah... I just stare at the screen now. I can't focus enough to actually write the script to make the sensors talk to the water pump. I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. \n\nI was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you only slept three or four hours, did you feel unusually upbeat or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really did. Before this whole low spell hit, I had this run where I was just... unusually fired up. Like, really upbeat, but also kind of on edge? I felt like I was totally on a roll with everything, but I'd get so easily annoyed if anyone interrupted me. I remember snapping at my wife because she asked me what I wanted for dinner while I was in the middle of re-wiring my pedalboard. \n\nI actually use this really specific power supply for my pedals, the Voodoo Lab Pedal Power 2 Plus. It's supposed to isolate the power to each pedal so you don't get that annoying ground loop hum. Hum is the worst when you're trying to record. I spent like three hours watching this documentary on Netflix about the history of electricity and the war of the currents between Edison and Tesla just to understand alternating current better. Tesla was such a genius, you know? He died completely broke in a hotel room in New York, feeding pigeons. He had this one specific white pigeon that he said he loved like a man loves a woman. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. Just miles and miles of conveyor belts moving at warp speed. He actually had to quit because lifting all those boxes completely messed up his lower back. \n\nBack pain is the absolute worst. I pulled a muscle in my lower back a few years ago just trying to lift a massive bag of dog food into the trunk of my car. We had a Golden Retriever back then. Best dog ever, his name was Barnaby. The engineering on those vacuums is actually pretty wild when you look into how the cyclonic separation works... \n\nBut yeah, anyway. No, I didn't ruin my life or go broke or anything. Just bought a few cables.\n\n[Last Question]\nAbout that energized, upbeat period when you needed less sleep and talked faster—did it last at least four days in a row?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it was... let's see. Was it four days? I'm trying to remember exactly how long that stretch was. I know it started on a Tuesday, because that's when the trash gets picked up in our neighborhood. The sanitation workers always come right at 6:00 AM, and they make this horrible crashing sound when they dump the bins. It's like they're actively trying to wake up the entire street. I actually tried recording that sound once too, hanging a microphone out my window, but the wind noise completely ruined the take. Wind is the absolute enemy of field recording. You need one of those giant fuzzy microphone covers, they call them "dead cats," which is a terrible name for a piece of audio gear when you think about it. I mean, who came up with that? It's kind of morbid. We don't have a cat, anyway. We just had Barnaby, the Golden Retriever I mentioned. Cats always make my eyes water. My sister has two of them, these little rescue tabbies, and every time I go to her place I have to take an antihistamine beforehand. She lives over in the Heights, which is getting so expensive now. They're tearing down all these beautiful old bungalows and putting up those giant, boxy modern houses that look like shipping containers... 

But wait, what were you asking? How many days? I'm honestly not totally sure if it was exactly four days in a row. It might have been, but it all kind of blends together when you're just going and going like that.

I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. \n\nI was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you only slept three or four hours, did you feel unusually upbeat or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really did. Before this whole low spell hit, I had this run where I was just... unusually fired up. Like, really upbeat, but also kind of on edge? I felt like I was totally on a roll with everything, but I'd get so easily annoyed if anyone interrupted me. I remember snapping at my wife because she asked me what I wanted for dinner while I was in the middle of re-wiring my pedalboard. \n\nI actually use this really specific power supply for my pedals, the Voodoo Lab Pedal Power 2 Plus. It's supposed to isolate the power to each pedal so you don't get that annoying ground loop hum. Hum is the worst when you're trying to record. I spent like three hours watching this documentary on Netflix about the history of electricity and the war of the currents between Edison and Tesla just to understand alternating current better. Tesla was such a genius, you know? He died completely broke in a hotel room in New York, feeding pigeons. He had this one specific white pigeon that he said he loved like a man loves a woman. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. I mean, I don't know for sure. I really couldn't tell you if it was exactly four days straight. Time just gets weird when you're not sleeping much, you know? Like, the days just kind of blur into one long continuous thing. It's like when you're binge-watching a show and suddenly the sun is coming up. I did that with this documentary series about the history of analog synthesizers. It was fascinating. They got into the whole Moog versus Buchla debate, which is basically the East Coast versus West Coast hip-hop rivalry but for nerds in the 70s. Bob Moog wanted everything to have a traditional keyboard so musicians could actually play it, but Don Buchla was all about these weird touch plates and sequencers. He didn't even want his instruments to be called synthesizers. He called them "electronic music boxes." Which is a cool name, honestly. It sounds like something you'd find in an antique shop run by a wizard. My wife loves antique shops. She dragged me to this one out in the country a few months ago, and the whole place smelled like dust and old paper. I actually found a stack of old reel-to-reel tapes there, but I didn't have a machine to play them on. Reel-to-reel machines are so expensive to maintain... 

But yeah, the days. I don't know if it was four days exactly. It's all just a blur.

And I've been noticing my jeans are starting to feel pretty loose lately, so... yeah, I guess that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. Most days lately, I just wake up with this really heavy, flat feeling. It's like... I don't know, like the apartment is just way too quiet, you know? Even picking up my guitar doesn't really lift me. \n\nActually, I bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but the action on it is incredible. The guy who sold it to me was this old blues player who claimed he once jammed with B.B. King. I mean, who knows if that's true, but he sure could play. He showed me this crazy pentatonic lick that I still practice sometimes. Or, well, I used to practice it. Lately, it just sits in the corner on its stand, collecting dust. But yeah, to answer your question, I've definitely been feeling pretty down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to die or hurt yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't really thought about anything like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I don't want to hurt myself. \n\nMy wife actually asks me that sometimes, especially when I get in these funks. She's a nurse, actually, over at the pediatric clinic on 4th street. It's a really nice facility, they just renovated the whole waiting room with this under-the-sea theme. They have these big murals of sea turtles and coral reefs, and there's this one huge octopus that takes up a whole wall. The artist who painted it is local, I think she has a studio in the arts district. We went to an art walk down there last summer and saw some of her other stuff, mostly landscapes, but really vibrant colors. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any thoughts about dying. Just... just feeling really flat.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Um, well, this current low stretch has been going on for about three weeks now. I guess it kind of started right after my wife settled into her new full-time work schedule. \n\nShe used to work part-time, just three days a week, which was nice because we'd have these long weekends together. We actually used to take these little road trips up to the mountains on her days off. There's this one cabin we rented a couple times near Big Bear. The guy who owns it built it himself back in the 70s, and it has this incredible wraparound deck made of redwood. He told us he sourced all the timber locally, which is pretty cool. We'd sit out there in the mornings with our coffee and just watch the Steller's jays hopping around in the pines. They're such loud birds, you know? Like, they have this really harsh call, almost like a screech, but their blue feathers are just so striking against the green trees. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, she's full-time now, five days a week, so she's gone a lot more. And that's... that's right around when this started, about three weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your low mood is mainly because your wife is gone more now?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? It's hard to say if it's *mainly* because of that. I definitely miss having her around as much, but I don't know if that's the whole reason. \n\nIt is weird being in the apartment by myself all day, though. We live in this complex that was built back in the 60s, and it's got this really interesting mid-century modern architecture. The roofline has these sharp angles, and there's this central courtyard with a pool that nobody ever uses. The pool guy comes every Tuesday, like clockwork. His name is Dave, older guy, always wears this faded Dodgers hat. He told me once he went to the World Series back in '88, the year Kirk Gibson hit that famous home run. He said the stadium just absolutely erupted, like you could feel the concrete shaking under your feet. I've never been to a baseball game myself, honestly. Just never really got into sports. My dad tried to get me into Little League when I was a kid, but I was always more interested in drawing in the dirt in the outfield. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, my wife's schedule. Yeah, I'm not totally sure if that's the main cause of the low mood, to be honest. It just happened around the same time.\n\nDoctor: If your wife were home with you more again, do you think your mood would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know if her being home would just instantly fix it, honestly. It's like... most days lately, I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling. Even if she was here, the apartment just feels too quiet. I can't even pick up my guitar to try and lift my mood. \n\nI actually bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but it has this really beautiful sunburst finish. The guy who sold it to me, he was missing his left index finger. He said he lost it in a woodworking accident back in the 90s when he was building custom cabinets. He still played, though! He showed me this crazy slide technique he developed to compensate for the missing finger. It sounded kind of like old Delta blues, you know? Robert Johnson style. I tried looking up some tutorials on YouTube to learn how to play with a slide, but you have to use this special glass or metal tube on your finger, and it just clanks against the frets when I try it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... even the guitar doesn't really help right now. Just this heavy feeling every morning, whether she's here or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It's been super frustrating. Like, when I try to work on some code or even arrange a new song on the computer, I just get stuck. I'll keep rereading the exact same line of code, or replaying the same eight bars of music over and over, and I just can't decide what to do next. My brain just kind of stalls out. \n\nSpeaking of coding, I actually started learning Python a couple years ago because I wanted to build this automated watering system for my wife's houseplants. She has this massive Monstera plant that she named 'Seymour' after that movie Little Shop of Horrors. Have you seen that? The original one with Rick Moranis is a classic, but the Broadway musical version has this totally different ending where the plant actually eats everybody and takes over the world. It's way darker. Anyway, Seymour the plant is huge, and I bought all these little moisture sensors on Amazon to stick in the soil. They were supposed to connect to a Raspberry Pi mini-computer, which is this tiny little circuit board that costs like thirty bucks. The guy who invented them actually wanted to make computing affordable for kids in developing countries, which is such a noble idea. \n\nBut yeah... I just stare at the screen now. I can't focus enough to actually write the script to make the sensors talk to the water pump. I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. \n\nI was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you only slept three or four hours, did you feel unusually upbeat or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really did. Before this whole low spell hit, I had this run where I was just... unusually fired up. Like, really upbeat, but also kind of on edge? I felt like I was totally on a roll with everything, but I'd get so easily annoyed if anyone interrupted me. I remember snapping at my wife because she asked me what I wanted for dinner while I was in the middle of re-wiring my pedalboard. \n\nI actually use this really specific power supply for my pedals, the Voodoo Lab Pedal Power 2 Plus. It's supposed to isolate the power to each pedal so you don't get that annoying ground loop hum. Hum is the worst when you're trying to record. I spent like three hours watching this documentary on Netflix about the history of electricity and the war of the currents between Edison and Tesla just to understand alternating current better. Tesla was such a genius, you know? He died completely broke in a hotel room in New York, feeding pigeons. He had this one specific white pigeon that he said he loved like a man loves a woman. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. I felt great, but I was definitely irritable.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you find yourself talking a lot more than usual or feeling like you couldn't stop talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, my wife pointed that out. She told me I was talking way faster than usual. Like, I'd be going on and on, jumping from talking about these new audio plugins I wanted to buy, straight into this business plan I had for a local artist collective, and then onto something else. I could kind of tell I wasn't really giving her much room to answer or even get a word in. \n\nSpeaking of local artists, there's this guy down the street who does these massive metal sculptures out of old car parts. He's got this one in his front yard that looks like a giant praying mantis made entirely out of Volkswagen Beetle fenders. It's actually really impressive. He told me he sources all the parts from this junkyard out by the highway. Junkyards are fascinating places, honestly. You walk through them and it's just rows and rows of these rusted-out machines that used to be someone's pride and joy, you know? Someone probably drove that Beetle off the lot in 1972, feeling like a million bucks, and now it's a bug leg in some guy's yard. I actually used to drive a Beetle when I was in my early twenties. It was this awful mustard yellow color, and the heater was permanently stuck on, which was miserable in the summer. I tried to fix it myself once, watched a bunch of YouTube tutorials, but I ended up snapping a cable and had to drive with the windows down for two years... \n\nBut yeah, I was definitely talking a lot. Just moving from one thing to the next, and my wife was just kind of staring at me trying to keep up.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you hospitalized?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wasn't hospitalized or anything like that. I mean, I was just at home, you know, working on my stuff. Hospitals kind of freak me out anyway, honestly. The lighting in those places is always so terrible. It's that awful fluorescent buzz, you know? It hums at like 60 hertz, which is right around a B-flat, and it just gets stuck in my head. I actually sampled a fluorescent light once for this ambient track I was making. I pitched it down two octaves and ran it through a massive reverb, and it sounded like this incredible drone. Reverb is such a powerful tool. My favorite is this old spring reverb tank I pulled out of a broken Hammond organ. Those organs are beasts. They weigh like 400 pounds. My buddy and I tried to move one up a flight of stairs once, and we almost dropped it on my foot. If that thing had landed on my toe, I probably would have ended up in the hospital, ironically enough. But no, for the energy thing, I didn't go to the hospital. I was just at the house.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, financially, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: No, no serious problems. Like I said, my wife got a little annoyed with me snapping at her and talking so fast, but it wasn't like a massive relationship-ending blowout or anything. We're solid. And I'm not working right now anyway, so there was no job to mess up. Financially... I mean, no. I didn't drain the bank account or anything crazy. I bought a few small things online, mostly just some patch cables and a couple of used effects pedals off Reverb.com. \n\nReverb is such a dangerous website if you're a musician, honestly. You can just scroll for hours looking at vintage gear. I found this guy selling an original 1980s Roland Juno-60 synthesizer, which is like the holy grail of that lush, chorus-heavy 80s sound. I didn't buy it, but I was looking at it. The shipping on it was insane, though. Like two hundred bucks just to ship it from Chicago. I really don't understand how shipping rates get calculated these days. I had a buddy who worked for UPS for a while, and he said the sorting facilities are just absolute chaos. Just miles and miles of conveyor belts moving at warp speed. He actually had to quit because lifting all those boxes completely messed up his lower back. \n\nBack pain is the absolute worst. I pulled a muscle in my lower back a few years ago just trying to lift a massive bag of dog food into the trunk of my car. We had a Golden Retriever back then. Best dog ever, his name was Barnaby. But man, the shedding... you'd find golden hair in your food, in your shoes, everywhere. We had to buy this special vacuum just to keep up with it. It was a Dyson, one of those ball ones. The engineering on those vacuums is actually pretty wild when you look into how the cyclonic separation works... \n\nBut yeah, anyway. No, I didn't ruin my life or go broke or anything. Just bought a few cables.\n\nDoctor: About that energized, upbeat period when you needed less sleep and talked faster—did it last at least four days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it was... let's see. The sanitation workers always come right at 6:00 AM, and they make this horrible crashing sound when they dump the bins. It's like they're actively trying to wake up the entire street. I actually tried recording that sound once too, hanging a microphone out my window, but the wind noise completely ruined the take. Wind is the absolute enemy of field recording. You need one of those giant fuzzy microphone covers, they call them \"dead cats,\" which is a terrible name for a piece of audio gear when you think about it. I mean, who came up with that? It's kind of morbid. We don't have a cat, anyway. We just had Barnaby, the Golden Retriever I mentioned. Cats always make my eyes water. I really couldn't tell you if it was exactly four days straight. Time just gets weird when you're not sleeping much, you know? Like, the days just kind of blur into one long continuous thing. It's like when you're binge-watching a show and suddenly the sun is coming up. I did that with this documentary series about the history of analog synthesizers. It was fascinating. They got into the whole Moog versus Buchla debate, which is basically the East Coast versus West Coast hip-hop rivalry but for nerds in the 70s. Bob Moog wanted everything to have a traditional keyboard so musicians could actually play it, but Don Buchla was all about these weird touch plates and sequencers. He didn't even want his instruments to be called synthesizers. He called them \"electronic music boxes.\" Which is a cool name, honestly. It sounds like something you'd find in an antique shop run by a wizard. My wife loves antique shops. She dragged me to this one out in the country a few months ago, and the whole place smelled like dust and old paper. It's all just a blur.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Reverb is such a powerful tool. My favorite is this old spring reverb tank I pulled out of a broken Hammond organ. Those organs are beasts. They weigh like 400 pounds. My buddy and I tried to move one up a flight of stairs once, and we almost dropped it on my foot. Oh, yeah, absolutely. I was doing way more than usual. I started making all these massive lists of musicians to contact, sending out messages, building these elaborate pitch decks... I actually learned how to use Keynote just for that. I mean, I have a Mac, and Mac is just so much better for creative stuff anyway. I really don't get why anyone uses Windows for audio production.

Like, they have this really harsh call, almost like a screech, but their blue feathers are just so striking against the green trees. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, she's full-time now, five days a week, so she's gone a lot more. And that's... that's right around when this started, about three weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your low mood is mainly because your wife is gone more now?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? It's hard to say if it's *mainly* because of that. I definitely miss having her around as much, but I don't know if that's the whole reason. \n\nIt is weird being in the apartment by myself all day, though. We live in this complex that was built back in the 60s, and it's got this really interesting mid-century modern architecture. The roofline has these sharp angles, and there's this central courtyard with a pool that nobody ever uses. The pool guy comes every Tuesday, like clockwork. His name is Dave, older guy, always wears this faded Dodgers hat. He told me once he went to the World Series back in '88, the year Kirk Gibson hit that famous home run. He said the stadium just absolutely erupted, like you could feel the concrete shaking under your feet. I've never been to a baseball game myself, honestly. Just never really got into sports. My dad tried to get me into Little League when I was a kid, but I was always more interested in drawing in the dirt in the outfield. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, my wife's schedule. Yeah, I'm not totally sure if that's the main cause of the low mood, to be honest. It just happened around the same time.\n\nDoctor: If your wife were home with you more again, do you think your mood would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know if her being home would just instantly fix it, honestly. It's like... most days lately, I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling. Even if she was here, the apartment just feels too quiet. I can't even pick up my guitar to try and lift my mood. \n\nI actually bought that guitar at this little pawn shop downtown. It's a Fender Stratocaster, Mexican made, but it has this really beautiful sunburst finish. The guy who sold it to me, he was missing his left index finger. He said he lost it in a woodworking accident back in the 90s when he was building custom cabinets. He still played, though! He showed me this crazy slide technique he developed to compensate for the missing finger. It sounded kind of like old Delta blues, you know? Robert Johnson style. I tried looking up some tutorials on YouTube to learn how to play with a slide, but you have to use this special glass or metal tube on your finger, and it just clanks against the frets when I try it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... even the guitar doesn't really help right now. Just this heavy feeling every morning, whether she's here or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It's been super frustrating. Like, when I try to work on some code or even arrange a new song on the computer, I just get stuck. I'll keep rereading the exact same line of code, or replaying the same eight bars of music over and over, and I just can't decide what to do next. My brain just kind of stalls out. \n\nSpeaking of coding, I actually started learning Python a couple years ago because I wanted to build this automated watering system for my wife's houseplants. She has this massive Monstera plant that she named 'Seymour' after that movie Little Shop of Horrors. Have you seen that? The original one with Rick Moranis is a classic, but the Broadway musical version has this totally different ending where the plant actually eats everybody and takes over the world. It's way darker. Anyway, Seymour the plant is huge, and I bought all these little moisture sensors on Amazon to stick in the soil. They were supposed to connect to a Raspberry Pi mini-computer, which is this tiny little circuit board that costs like thirty bucks. The guy who invented them actually wanted to make computing affordable for kids in developing countries, which is such a noble idea. \n\nBut yeah... I just stare at the screen now. I can't focus enough to actually write the script to make the sensors talk to the water pump. I just read the same line and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, not right now, but... there was a stretch a little while ago where, yeah, I really didn't need much sleep at all. For several nights in a row, I think I only slept maybe three or four hours? And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, completely energized. It honestly felt like sleep was just this annoying thing getting in the way of me finishing the next track I was working on, or this massive spreadsheet I was building. \n\nI was actually making this spreadsheet to track the vintage synthesizer market. I got really obsessed with the Roland Juno-60. It's this incredible analog synth from the 80s. The prices on Reverb dot com have just skyrocketed over the last five years. I was trying to map out the price fluctuations based on the serial numbers and the condition of the wooden side panels. Those side panels are notorious for getting chipped, you know? They're made of this particle board with a wood veneer, and if someone drops it even an inch, the corner just crumples. My buddy Eric had one in college, and he actually tried to replace the panels with solid walnut that he cut himself. He bought this vintage table saw at an estate sale, but the blade was super dull and it ended up burning the wood as it cut. It smelled like a campfire in his garage for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I was just staying up all night working on that spreadsheet and the music, and I felt like I didn't even need to sleep. It was just a few hours a night, but I felt totally fine.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you only slept three or four hours, did you feel unusually upbeat or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really did. Before this whole low spell hit, I had this run where I was just... unusually fired up. Like, really upbeat, but also kind of on edge? I felt like I was totally on a roll with everything, but I'd get so easily annoyed if anyone interrupted me. I remember snapping at my wife because she asked me what I wanted for dinner while I was in the middle of re-wiring my pedalboard. \n\nI actually use this really specific power supply for my pedals, the Voodoo Lab Pedal Power 2 Plus. It's supposed to isolate the power to each pedal so you don't get that annoying ground loop hum. Hum is the worst when you're trying to record. I spent like three hours watching this documentary on Netflix about the history of electricity and the war of the currents between Edison and Tesla just to understand alternating current better. Tesla was such a genius, you know? He died completely broke in a hotel room in New York, feeding pigeons. He had this one specific white pigeon that he said he loved like a man loves a woman. It's kind of tragic, really, how someone so brilliant could end up so isolated. My uncle actually used to breed racing pigeons in his backyard. He had this massive coop... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, I was just super fired up back then, and if anyone broke my focus, I would just snap at them. I felt great, but I was definitely irritable.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you find yourself talking a lot more than usual or feeling like you couldn't stop talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, my wife pointed that out. She told me I was talking way faster than usual. Like, I'd be going on and on, jumping from talking about these new audio plugins I wanted to buy, straight into this business plan I had for a local artist collective, and then onto something else. I could kind of tell I wasn't really giving her much room to answer or even get a word in. \n\nSpeaking of local artists, there's this guy down the street who does these massive metal sculptures out of old car parts. He's got this one in his front yard that looks like a giant praying mantis made entirely out of Volkswagen Beetle fenders. It's actually really impressive. He told me he sources all the parts from this junkyard out by the highway. Junkyards are fascinating places, honestly. You walk through them and it's just rows and rows of these rusted-out machines that used to be someone's pride and joy, you know? Someone probably drove that Beetle off the lot in 1972, feeling like a million bucks, and now it's a bug leg in some guy's yard. I actually used to drive a Beetle when I was in my early twenties. It was this awful mustard yellow color, and the heater was permanently stuck on, which was miserable in the summer. I tried to fix it myself once, watched a bunch of YouTube tutorials, but I ended up snapping a cable and had to drive with the windows down for two years... \n\nBut yeah, I was definitely talking a lot. Just moving from one thing to the next, and my wife was just kind of staring at me trying to keep up.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you hospitalized?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wasn't hospitalized or anything like that. I mean, I was just at home, you know, working on my stuff. Hospitals kind of freak me out anyway, honestly. The lighting in those places is always so terrible. It's that awful fluorescent buzz, you know? It hums at like 60 hertz, which is right around a B-flat, and it just gets stuck in my head. I actually sampled a fluorescent light once for this ambient track I was making. I pitched it down two octaves and ran it through a massive reverb, and it sounded like this incredible drone. Reverb is such a powerful tool. My favorite is this old spring reverb tank I pulled out of a broken Hammond organ. Those organs are beasts. They weigh like 400 pounds. My buddy and I tried to move one up a flight of stairs once, and we almost dropped it on my foot. If that thing had landed on my toe, I probably would have ended up in the hospital, ironically enough. But no, for the energy thing, I didn't go to the hospital. I was just at the house.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, financially, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: No, no serious problems. Like I said, my wife got a little annoyed with me snapping at her and talking so fast, but it wasn't like a massive relationship-ending blowout or anything. We're solid. And I'm not working right now anyway, so there was no job to mess up. Financially... I mean, no. I didn't drain the bank account or anything crazy. I bought a few small things online, mostly just some patch cables and a couple of used effects pedals off Reverb.com. \n\nReverb is such a dangerous website if you're a musician, honestly. You can just scroll for hours looking at vintage gear. I found this guy selling an original 1980s Roland Juno-60 synthesizer, which is like the holy grail of that lush, chorus-heavy 80s sound. I didn't buy it, but I was looking at it. The shipping on it was insane, though. Like two hundred bucks just to ship it from Chicago. I really don't understand how shipping rates get calculated these days. I had a buddy who worked for UPS for a while, and he said the sorting facilities are just absolute chaos. Just miles and miles of conveyor belts moving at warp speed. He actually had to quit because lifting all those boxes completely messed up his lower back. \n\nBack pain is the absolute worst. The engineering on those vacuums is actually pretty wild when you look into how the cyclonic separation works... \n\nBut yeah, anyway. No, I didn't ruin my life or go broke or anything. They got into the whole Moog versus Buchla debate, which is basically the East Coast versus West Coast hip-hop rivalry but for nerds in the 70s. Bob Moog wanted everything to have a traditional keyboard so musicians could actually play it, but Don Buchla was all about these weird touch plates and sequencers. He didn't even want his instruments to be called synthesizers. He called them \"electronic music boxes.\" Which is a cool name, honestly. You can just scroll for hours looking at vintage gear. I found this guy selling an original 1980s Roland Juno-60 synthesizer, which is like the holy grail of that lush, chorus-heavy 80s sound. I didn't buy it, but I was looking at it. The shipping on it was insane, though. Like two hundred bucks just to ship it from Chicago. I really don't understand how shipping rates get calculated these days. I had a buddy who worked for UPS for a while, and he said the sorting facilities are just absolute chaos. Just miles and miles of conveyor belts moving at warp speed. He actually had to quit because lifting all those boxes completely messed up his lower back. \n\nBack pain is the absolute worst. The engineering on those vacuums is actually pretty wild when you look into how the cyclonic separation works... \n\nBut yeah, anyway. No, I didn't ruin my life or go broke or anything. I actually tried recording that sound once too, hanging a microphone out my window, but the wind noise completely ruined the take. Wind is the absolute enemy of field recording. You need one of those giant fuzzy microphone covers, they call them \"dead cats,\" which is a terrible name for a piece of audio gear when you think about it. I mean, who came up with that? It's kind of morbid. We don't have a cat, anyway. We just had Barnaby, the Golden Retriever I mentioned. Cats always make my eyes water. My sister has two of them, these little rescue tabbies, and every time I go to her place I have to take an antihistamine beforehand. She lives over in the Heights, which is getting so expensive now. They're tearing down all these beautiful old bungalows and putting up those giant, boxy modern houses that look like shipping containers... \n\nBut wait, what were you asking? How many days? I really couldn't tell you if it was exactly four days straight. Time just gets weird when you're not sleeping much, you know? Like, the days just kind of blur into one long continuous thing. It's like when you're binge-watching a show and suddenly the sun is coming up. I did that with this documentary series about the history of analog synthesizers. It was fascinating. They got into the whole Moog versus Buchla debate, which is basically the East Coast versus West Coast hip-hop rivalry but for nerds in the 70s. Bob Moog wanted everything to have a traditional keyboard so musicians could actually play it, but Don Buchla was all about these weird touch plates and sequencers. He didn't even want his instruments to be called synthesizers. He called them \"electronic music boxes.\" Which is a cool name, honestly. It sounds like something you'd find in an antique shop run by a wizard. My wife loves antique shops. She dragged me to this one out in the country a few months ago, and the whole place smelled like dust and old paper. I actually found a stack of old reel-to-reel tapes there, but I didn't have a machine to play them on. Reel-to-reel machines are so expensive to maintain... \n\nBut yeah, the days. I really don't get why anyone uses Windows for audio production. \n\nThe latency issues with Windows audio drivers are just a complete nightmare. Nobody else can make a laptop trackpad that actually feels good to use. My buddy Dave has a PC laptop and the trackpad feels like you're dragging your finger across a piece of sandpaper.
